LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

China is making ground on Taiwan in Southeast Asia. The issue swam into view last month. First, the Indonesian navy performed a joint exercise with the Chinese navy off the east coast of Taiwan—which it then hastily downplayed. A few days later, on Aug. 16, Al Jazeera aired an interview with Malaysian Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim, in which he made comments suggesting sympathy with China taking military action against Taiwan. “I always take Malaysia as an example. If one province decides to break away, do we use force? I think I will protect the sanctity and unity of the nation,” Anwar said when asked if he would condemn China using force against Taiwan. Anwar subsequently walked back these comments, emphasizing that a peaceful approach was best. However, while his comments were unusually strong, they reflect a slow drift from Malaysia toward Beijing’s line on the matter. Just compare Malaysia and China’s joint statements past and present. One in 2014 affirmed that “Taiwan is an inseparable part of China” but also pointedly mentioned support for “peaceful reunification.” But in 2024 and again in 2025 , there was no specific mention of peace. Other countries have also dropped mentions of peace. Joint statements with Cambodia included it last in 2019 but not since 2022 . Thailand has also subtly shifted its position. In 2023 , statements simply recognized “Taiwan as an inalienable part of China.” In 2025 , new language appeared declaring that Thailand would not “support any call for the independence of Taiwan,” and in 2026 , it “firmly supported peaceful reunification.” The Philippines, while keeping to the “one China” policy, has usually taken a stiffer line on potential force against Taiwan. This reflects its closeness to the United States and its own strong stance against China in the South China Sea. However, this year has seen President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. soften somewhat. Subscribe now to support our journalism. Not so long ago, it seemed that the world was becoming more civilized. The end of the Cold War was supposed to bring the triumph of the rules-based order. Enlightened norms and cooperation, buttressed by a broad-minded superpower, would supplant anarchic patterns of the past. It was an inspiring vision—and, alas, an illusion. Now, all around the world, rules are in retreat. Aggression and conquest are surging; economic coercion and predation are rampant. Respect for international law and organizations is cratering; arms control is in decline. Great powers are reviving dreams of empire; the United States is tempted to seek narrow national aggrandizement at the cost of world order. Our world is at risk of becoming a gangster’s paradise, in which rules matter far less than they once did—and raw power matters more. This is a harsh shift for those whose mindsets were made in a kinder moment. It will present severe tests for leaders who must navigate an unforgiving age. * How to Write Better Rules for Global Finance
  Foreign Policy | 09 Sep 04:02 UTC
  The importance of a “common good” compass.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . Subscribe now to support our journalism. This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . Subscribe now to support our journalism. A world without rules is a frightening prospect. But before we mourn the passing of the old order, we should be honest about what its rules were designed to do. The architecture that has governed the global economy for the past 80 years was never a neutral system serving everyone equally. The Bretton Woods system, created in 1944, established institutions such as the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and the World Bank to protect and promote peace, stability, and development, yet it encoded the interests of a small group of wealthy nations from the start. What is fraying today, amid resurgent nationalism and geopolitical tensions of a kind not seen since World War II, is not the rule of law in the global economy; it is the legitimacy of rules that socialized risks while privatizing rewards and that gave a majority of the world’s countries—and people—little voice in the decisions that shaped their futures. The task ahead is therefore not to restore the old order, or to resign ourselves to a lawless vacuum, but to write new rules around a different organizing principle: the common good. * A New Engine of Development
  Foreign Policy | 09 Sep 04:01 UTC
  The era of easy growth is over, but rich and poor countries can still join forces.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . Subscribe now to support our journalism. This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . This article appears in the Fall 2026 print issue: How to Survive in a World Without Rules . Subscribe now to support our journalism. In the mid-1980s, Bangladesh seemed an unlikely candidate for industrial takeoff. It was desperately poor, had explosive population growth, and exported barely over $30 million in simple garments a year. But the global economy offered a ladder. Europe had already abolished tariffs on Bangladesh-made clothing, while U.S. quotas on other Asian exporters pushed retailers to seek new suppliers. Western brands hunting for cheap production showered Bangladesh with orders. Those orders, in turn, unlocked bank credit to build and expand. Entrepreneurs could rent a floor, install sewing machines, hire workers, and start exporting; South Korea’s Daewoo came in and trained Bangladesh’s first generation of garment industry managers. By 2022, garment exports had risen more than a thousandfold, surpassing $40 billion a year, and the industry employed roughly 4 million people. Since 1990, annual income of the average Bangladeshi has risen from under $300 to about $2,600. One generation later, Ethiopia tried to climb the same ladder but found its rungs broken. The country borrowed $1 billion abroad, spending most of it to build six new industrial parks . By mid-2021, all of Ethiopia’s parks together employed only about 90,000 people in a country where 2 million new workers enter the labor force each year. Bangladesh, China, and Vietnam already dominated labor-intensive manufacturing, while India and the Philippines had captured much of the low-cost services trade. Automation, meanwhile, was reducing the value of cheap labor. Because the new factories imported most of their inputs from abroad, few local suppliers developed. All the while, the parks carried nearly $500 million in scheduled interest payments. The triple hit of the COVID-19 pandemic, civil war, and new U.S. tariffs drove out investors and shuttered businesses. Ethiopia defaulted on its debt. Income per person remains around $1,000 a year. I n the mid-1980s, Bangladesh seemed an unlikely candidate for industrial takeoff. I
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/09/economic-development-global-south-over-growth-globalization-jobs-population-demographics-labor-force/

Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. Last year, we explored what life is like for soldiers in the People’s Liberation Army. This week, in lieu of our usual news coverage, we’re turning our attention to the officer corps and what their jobs entail—especially in some of the Chinese military’s more unique roles. Chinese President Xi Jinping is married to a major general in the People’s Liberation Army (PLA)—sort of. Xi’s second wife, Peng Liyuan, is a famous singer who joined the PLA’s entertainment corps in 1980, at the age of 18. She spent her career working within the military’s vast entertainment apparatus, which performs for troops as well as operating record labels, film studios, and other commercial media businesses. Peng never commanded soldiers or wielded a weapon, yet when she was appointed head of the PLA Academy of Art in 2012, she was granted the formal military rank of major general—a title commonly given to entertainers who work for the military. Comedian Huang Hong, for instance, was also made a major general in 2012 after being hired to run the PLA’s August First Film Studio. These examples are a reminder of just how sprawling the concept of an officer can be in the world’s largest military, where they perform an array of jobs that either don’t exist in Western militaries or are usually handled by civilian contractors. China has spent decades trying to simplify the officer corps system and reduce the number of personnel with military ranks, but it is still a mess. (I won’t even get into China’s paramilitary forces .) There are two significant distinctions within the PLA officer corps: the split between command and technical staff; and the division within the command track between regular commanders and political officers, which was formalized in 2021. As in Western militaries, PLA command officers generally enter through universities or military academies, and those promoted from enlisted ranks are typically sent to military academies before advancing further. Regular commanders are responsible for battlefield command and military readiness, as in the traditional Western view of a military officer. The latter is especially impo
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/08/china-pla-officer-corps-military-ccp/

And at the forefront of that effort are mining engineers, who oversee the lifecycle of mines and can take on careers in areas ranging from mine design and safety to environmental specialties. The United States is currently home to 14 mining schools, down from 25 in the 1980s. Those schools collectively graduate between 150 and 200 mining engineers a year, which doesn’t even meet the current demand for graduates, said Aaron Noble, the head of the mining and minerals engineering department at Virginia Tech. For decades, European policy toward Israel has largely deferred to Washington. The 27 EU member states are deeply divided. Historically, a minority, led by Ireland, has strongly favored Palestinian independence, while a bigger group, led by Germany, has unwaveringly stood by Israel, with others shifting according to their government. * South Korea Should Help Defend Ukraine’s Skies
  Foreign Policy | 08 Sep 13:15 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Understanding the conflict four years on. In July, the deadliest month for Ukrainian civilians since April 2022, Ukraine intercepted only 29 of Russia’s 195 incoming ballistic missiles, according to the Ukrainian Defense Ministry. As Russia intensifies its ballistic missile attacks, Ukraine is running dangerously low on the interceptors needed to defend against them. Neither of its major sources of support appears willing and able to fill the gap: European allies have already drawn heavily on their own air defense stocks, and the United States continues to deplete its own missile-defense interceptor inventories in its war against Iran. * Q&A: What can – and cannot – be said about global warming’s role in the 2026 Himalayan floods
  Carbon Brief | 08 Sep 16:03 UTC
  On the morning of 26 August, flash floods surged through a Himalayan border region of Nepal and the Chinese region… The post Q&A: What can – and cannot – be said about global warming’s role in the 2026 Himalayan floods appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: On the morning of 26 August, flash floods surged through a Himalayan border region of Nepal and the Chinese region of Tibet, killing more than 1,300 people , with thousands still missing. In the days since the floods, scientists have examined satellite imagery, drone footage and seismic data in order to understand and explain the forces behind the event. While initial theories pinned the flood on a glacial collapse, scientists now understand the event as a “ multi-hazard cascade ”, which began with a bedrock collapse. Some climate sceptics have tried to use this to falsely claim that human-caused climate change had no impact on the event. Yet, scientists have noted that, while no formal attribution study has been carried out thus far, warming is making such ice-rock avalanches in the region more likely. Researchers have highlighted how rapid warming is dramatically reshaping Asia’s high-mountain region – and identified rising temperatures, glacier retreat and permafrost thaw as factors that may have all contributed to the disaster. Balendra Shah, Nepal’s prime minister, has called the floods a “serious signal that…the risks we must bear in the Himalayan region are increasing” due to climate change. Here, Carbon Brief unpacks what scientists currently know about the causes of the catastrophic event and what they can – and cannot – say about the role of climate change. A report published on 28 August by the HiRisk scientific consortium of high mountain experts detailed the events that led to the flash floods. It said that events were set in motion on 26 August when a mass of bedrock, as well as the glacier ice on top of it, broke off a slope of Langtang-Lirung mountain in the Nepalese Himalaya, plunging from approximately 5,200 metres above sea level to the valley floor at 3,000 metres. The landslide shook the ground hard enough that, at 8:37am Nepal local time, the US Geological Survey (USGS) initially reported a magnitude 4.4 earthquake. Later that day , it clarified the shaking was caused by glacier collapse and debris flow, equivalent to a magnitude 5.2 earthquake. On the valley floor, the melting ice, water and debris slammed into the Lhende Khola river, a high-altitude river that runs along Nepal’s border with China. Known downstream as the Bhote Koshi river in Nepal and the Poiqu or Poqu in China, the Lhende Khole feeds a network of rivers across Nepal and the Chinese region of Tibet, including the Trishuli river. (In China, the Lhende Khola is known 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/qa-what-can-and-cannot-be-said-about-global-warmings-role-in-the-2026-himalayan-floods

--- Longevity (4) ---

* The Adaptive Cost Hypothesis of Aging, a Viewpoint on Antagonist Pleiotropy
  Fight Aging | 08 Sep 18:22 UTC
  At the high level, without delving into specific mechanisms, the consensus view on aging is that it exists in the shadow of natural selection. * Hurricane Helene Washed His Wife Away From Their Mobile Home. Now New Residents Live on the Same Land.
  Longreads | 08 Sep 16:31 UTC
  "Residents of a riverfront trailer park have lost homes and loved ones in multiple storms. A policy gap puts mobile home residents in harm’s way again and again."
  FULL TEXT: Dave Masters watched as his father’s house careened into a telephone pole and split in two, after Hurricane Frances lifted it from its foundation in 2004. Hayward County, in Western North Carolina, bought the house and declared the patch near the town of Clyde “permanent open space under a federally funded program to reduce the risk of future loss of life and property damage.” Not all homes were treated equally, though, in the aftermath of distaster. Hurricanes Frances, Ivan, and Helene, along with Tropical Storm Fred, all brought heavy rains and flooding to the nearby Hyder Mountain Road mobile home park. They’re “part the result of a gap in disaster policy that allows mobile home residents to be put in harm’s way again and again.” For The Assembly , in a piece produced for ProPublica ’s Local Reporting Network, Ren Larson explains why. The federal government’s programs to buy out disaster-prone buildings are “the cornerstone of the Nation’s system for emergency management,” according to the Federal Emergency Management Agency. But the program was developed with traditional homeowners in mind, and mobile homes, the country’s largest source of affordable housing, are often left out. Trailers, even when they become immovable and lifelong homes, aren’t typically considered real estate but instead personal property, like motorcycles and boats. That still leaves people living on flood-prone land. For the state to actually buy that land, mobile home park owners have to take the buyout, and FEMA’s program offers little incentive for them to do so: They can’t collect from the government for lost rent or for the value of the mobile homes on top of the land. Some park owners say the payouts are not high enough to allow them to relocate; others aren’t even aware of the buyout program. So park owners regularly allow new trailers to move into the vacant spots. Art and Lynne Heinmiller, who own the Hyder Mountain Road mobile home park, did not respond to questions — which were emailed, texted and mailed via certified letter — about allowing trailers and campers to move back to where they’d washed away “The storm destroyed the home where I grew up, and changed New Orleans forever.” “Windell Curole built a vast levee to protect his district from disappearing into the ocean—despite federal resistance to his plan.” “Appalachia’s ‘climate haven’ myth unravels.”

  FULL TEXT: “Beirut is broken, and so is my body,” wrote my friend Nay on Aug. 5, 2020, in a diary entry that she shared on Instagram. In a matter of seconds, Nay was propelled into the corridor. Her room was destroyed. The large glass window in front of her bed had exploded, and the ceiling had fallen in. Sharp shards of glass were scattered throughout the room. “It all just stopped in front of my feet. Nothing got to me, as if someone had shielded me. To this day, I can’t explain it.” Nay told me that the feeling was still with her, five years on: seeing her room, and her home, absolutely shattered. Her father appeared in front of her. As he was rushing to check on her, he had walked on the shattered glass that was all over the floor, and his feet were all bloody. The vehicle beneath him, a donated Ford Model T with a decade of delivering newspapers on the odometer, chattered with ease over alternating segments of fresh gravel and steaming asphalt that constituted Brazil’s new highway, connecting Rio de Janeiro with São Paulo. On both sides of the road, Oliveira surveyed the deforested Paraíba Valley, which had hosted the world’s first coffee boom – ranks of bean-fruiting bushes whose product had reached global markets in 60-kilo sacks on the backs of mules. Oliveira was among the first to see his nation at highway speed, and velocity could make the eroded landscapes of the past feel irrelevant. He believed he was chasing the future. He arrived in São Paulo to cheering crowds and a press corps keen to follow his automotive exploits. Oliveira’s rapid progress on this first leg, not to mention his countrymen’s enthusiastic support, fortified his resolve to push on to his final destination: the United States’ capital at Washington, DC. And indeed, Andersen has since been headcanoned, with great enthusiasm, as a 19th-century ‘disaster bi’. A disaster bisexual is less an identity or a diagnosis than a way of reading scenes that spill over. Messy. Earnest. Intensely sincere to the point of social detonation. Seen in modern drama from Bridgerton to Saltburn , they are characterised by living, loving and feeling in a perpetual crescendo, then come to a full stop when they melt down. The term circulates now as affectionate shorthand, especially online, for people and characters who come in too hot, overshare, and punctuate the resulting social chaos with wit. * A Mississippi Death Penalty Jury Was Seated. With One Black Juror.
  Longreads | 02 Sep 11:00 UTC
  "The jury sentenced Pitchford to death."
  FULL TEXT: Earlier this year, the US Supreme Court heard an appeal from death row inmate Terry Pitchford, who was denied relief by the Mississippi Supreme Court after his attorneys claimed Black people were illegally struck from his jury. Investigative reporter Hannah McAlilly delves into the case, and into the wider problem of racial prejudice in the selection of juries in Mississippi: A prosecutor struck a Black woman for having “red dyed hair” and in another, a prosecutor struck a Black man because he had “a ponytail, a sort of strange hair design.” That prosecutor told the judge he was concerned the juror might identify too closely with the defendant, who also was of a similar age. “Both have unusual hair styles,” the prosecutor said. Prosecutors struck Black jurors who, they said, seemed angry, irritated or inattentive. A Black woman was struck because the prosecutor didn’t like her “vibe.” Another, who worked a night shift before showing up to jury selection, was struck for being “fidgety” and looking “angry.” Another was struck because she “frowned a lot.” “Mister Anthony could be my grandfather, if my grandfather, a retired Memphis city bus driver, was still alive, still driving strangers from one stop to another.” “I’d heard that moan in the presence of older Southern black folk my entire life, but I’d never heard it connecting two rhymed verses.   "Devotion to a Florida home."
  FULL TEXT: In a beautiful and vivid essay on home, time, and family, Diane Roberts reflects on the house that her parents built, and their family’s land in Leon County, Florida, to which she feels deeply connected. Home and time are as tangled as the skunk vine and Confederate jasmine choking my backyard fence. I suppose that’s to be expected. My house holds our history and the indispensable relics of former houses, objects cherished by the now-dead: the tiny rocking chair my grandfather made for my mother when she was three; the 1960 TR3 in the barn my brother never quite got around to fixing; the two old rosebushes dug up from the Washington County farm, replanted at Great-Aunt Rilla’s house in Chipley, then dug up again and transported to our front yard. The house didn’t simply belong to us, it was us—our mother, our father, Brad, and me. “The writer and farmer on local knowledge, embracing limits, and the exploitation of rural America.” ‘My father designed two of the houses I grew up in. * The Patients Israel Won’t Let Leave Gaza
  New Lines | 02 Sep 10:09 UTC
  According to the WHO, more than 20,000 people in Gaza currently require medical evacuation. But reaching that treatment requires navigating a medical evacuation system in which weeks and months can pass as illnesses progress — and ultimately it is only Israeli approval that determines who can leave. The post The Patients Israel Won’t Let ...
  FULL TEXT: Every day, Ibrahim Abu Aram waited for a phone call telling him that his medical evacuation had finally been approved. A father and blacksmith from Gaza, Ibrahim had been diagnosed with pemphigus vulgaris, a rare autoimmune disease that causes painful blisters across the body. In a video recorded during the height of his illness, he said: “I used to work, but as the disease progressed, my whole life came to a standstill.” Years of treatment failed to stop the disease. After developing sepsis, his condition deteriorated further, leaving specialized care outside Gaza as his only remaining chance of survival. Together with his family, he spent four years seeking permission to leave Gaza for treatment, carrying two medical referrals as each delay put his life further at risk. In April 2026, Israel still had not approved his medical evacuation. Ibrahim died before he could leave Gaza for the treatment he needed. Ibrahim was far from alone. According to data from the World Health Organization (WHO), at least 1,092 patients registered for medical evacuation died in Gaza while waiting to leave between July 2024 and late November 2025. For many Palestinians in Gaza, survival depends not only on the urgency of a patient’s medical condition, but also on a chain of administrative decisions over which they have no control. As Israel’s genocide has devastated Gaza’s healthcare system, treatment abroad has become the only remaining option for many patients. According to the WHO, more than 20,000 people in Gaza currently require medical evacuation. But reaching that treatment means navigating a medical evacuation system in which weeks or months can pass as illnesses progress, and ultimately it is only Israeli approval that determines who can leave, a process that patients and humanitarian organizations describe as opaque, corrupt and unpredictable. Some, like Ibrahim, never receive permission in time. Medical evacuations out of Gaza long predate the current war. Before October 2023, patients requiring treatment unavailable in Gaza were routinely referred to hospitals in the West Bank, East Jerusalem and sometimes Israel. Palestinians with cancer, congenital conditions and other illnesses requiring specialized care had long been referred to hospitals outside the strip. Between 2019 and 2021, however, 35% of security-permit applications by patients in Gaza were either denied or delayed, according to WHO figures. In July 2023, 1 in 5 patient applications was denied or delaye
